MARKET VIEW
2026 Has, So Far, Defied the Traditional Midterm Year Playbook
Jordy Fuentes, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Current Landscape. To no surprise, market volatility persisted in 2026. Performance initially
tracked historical midterm election patterns before diverging meaningfully as the year
progressed, though there are still three months to go. Several items remain at hand as U.S.

voters head to the polls in November, including geopolitical and foreign policy uncertainty,
energy driven inflation pressures, affordability concerns, U.S. fiscal trajectory, and rapid
advancements in AI. Regardless of the eventual political outcome, history suggests that
markets and the economy can perform well across different configurations of government.
In our view, markets continue to be anchored by strong fundamentals and encouraging
seasonal trends.

To Be Addressed. U.S. domestic and foreign policy remain in focus as the global reorder,
tit-for-tat tariffs, and geopolitical conflicts spanning the Middle East to Eastern Europe have
added layers of uncertainty to the U.S. economic outlook.
At the same time, persistent geopolitical flare-ups have pushed oil prices materially
higher, adding to inflation pressures and keeping them above the Fed’s 2% target.
Headline Consumer Price Index (CPI) held above 3% year-over-year (YoY) in August, while

its core measure, which excludes food and energy, held at 2.4% YoY. The latest Personal
Consumption Expenditure (PCE), the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation, also remained
above 3% YoY for both its headline and core figures. 2
The U.S. fiscal trajectory is another area of focus. The U.S. budget deficit stands at 5.4% of
GDP , just above this century’s average of 4.6%. Meanwhile, U.S. public sector debt surpassed
the $40 trillion mark for the first time in August, oscillating around 100% of GDP .3 While these

trends warrant close monitoring, we view them as a slow-moving structural headwind rather
than an immediate concern, particularly within a highly innovative and diverse U.S. economy.
AI has emerged as a policy consideration, with debates increasingly centered on both
regulatory safeguards and the economic implications of the expanding infrastructure buildout.
For voters, those concerns extend to affordability, as the significant energy requirements of

new data centers raise questions about potential pressure on electricity costs.
Strap In for The Ride. Leading up to the midterm election, markets tend to experience
greater swings as investors navigate uncertainty surrounding the policy path for the
remainder of the presidential cycle (Exhibit 2A). Since 1930, the S&P 500 has averaged an
intrayear drawdown of 20% in midterm years, with the latest election in 2022 experiencing
a 25% intrayear pullback. This year, however, the S&P 500 largest peak-to-trough drawdown

has been 9%, considerably shallower than its long-term average (Exhibit 2B). Yet while
volatility may rise as election day approaches, 2026 has increasingly defied the traditional
midterm year playbook.
Why 2026 is Different. Despite entering a challenging stretch of the presidential cycle,
U.S. Equities have materially outpaced the typical year two pattern (Exhibit 2C). The S&P
500 experienced its best Q2 in a midterm year since 1938, surging 14.9% compared to an

average decline of 1.6%. From a monthly perspective, September stands out as the weakest
month, though month-to-date performance points to an unusual 1.0% rise. That resilience
follows a 2.6% gain in August, another month in which performance meaningfully exceeded
the historical decline of 0.5% (Exhibit 2D).
Through September, U.S. Equity resilience has also extended beyond headline returns with
both the frequency and magnitude of market pullbacks remaining relatively subdued. Market

corrections of 10% or more have occurred in just 29% of midterm election years since 1930.4
Against this backdrop, the market’s last correction came in spring 2025, while this year has
2 Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics. Data through August 31, 2026.
3 U.S. Treasury, Bureau of Economic Analysis. Data as of September 21, 2026.
4 Bloomberg, data through December 31, 2022.
Investment Implications
In our view, any related market
weakness could represent a

potential buying opportunity for
long-term investors.

5 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
seen only one 5%+ pullback compared to the average three per year, underscoring relatively
resilient market conditions.5 Year-to-date, the S&P 500 has increased 13%, well above the
average annual return of 3% according to Bloomberg through September 25, and on pace
to mark the strongest midterm election year since 2006. Taken together, 2026 has thus far
defied the traditionally weak midterm year seasonal pattern, reinforcing that every election

cycle is unique and historical trends may be a useful guide to navigate market volatility.
What’s Ahead? Forward performance is increasingly constructive for Equities. In the
post-war period, the S&P 500 rose 100% of the time in the six and 12 months following
elections, averaging 13% and 14%, respectively. 6 Even narrowing the time frame to three
months, markets ended higher 90% of the time. As investors push through the finish line,
the last three months of the year tend to be the strongest with October standing out,

ending higher 67% of the time. On average, Q4 has gained 5.6% since 1930, far surpassing
the preceding three quarters of election years. 7 Greater clarity about the U.S. fiscal policy
path serves as a potential tailwind for investors following the election.
To Take Home. Volatility tends to rise during midterm years, but history suggests that
reacting to short-term political uncertainty has rarely rewarded long-term investors. Over
longer horizons, the data suggests Equity returns and economic strength are shaped less

by election outcomes than by corporate profits, valuations, labor market conditions and
capital investments.

THE TICKER TAPE
What is the bond market telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
We believe the bond market is telling us that the Fed is on track to meeting their dual
mandate of price stability and full employment. More importantly, when examining the
full yield curve the 2-year Treasury yield is currently around 4.88%, which typically is
the most sensitive to the fed funds rate and tends to price in future potential policy

adjustments. At the same time, the 10-year Treasury yield is currently about 5.11%. This
leads us to believe that the market expects the Fed to engineer a couple of more hikes
while the back end of the curve is indicating that economic growth is still healthy.
Taken together, it appears the full curve, which has clearly flattened, believes the Fed is
doing its job, while supply shocks, for obvious reasons, are not leading to higher long-run
inflation. The backup in long-dated yields is growth, not necessarily inflation as indicated

by rising “real” yields. Therefore, we view the drift higher in yields as a more normal sign
than what many have originally suggested. We continue to emphasize higher economic
growth for longer, rising productivity in the medium term, supply shortages to remain in
key areas within the AI space, and shocks in the energy equation. We expect the energy
shocks to ultimately fade away over time, but we do not believe that growth fades
anytime soon. In our view, a more normal bond market pricing in Fed moves at the short

end, and growth at the long end helps financial conditions and economic growth operate
in a more durable manner.
In summary, the front-end of the curve expects 3 to 4 more hikes in the next 18 months
or so. This can be debated obviously based on future data especially short-term energy
supply shocks. Long-end yields are drifting higher based on five main factors below, in
our view.
1. Strong economic growth (both
services and manufacturing)
2. Hawkish Fed

3. Debt and deficit concerns
4. Lower demand for long duration
sovereign debt in general
5. Energy supply shocks
The largest weight being strong economic activity which kick starts portfolio
repositioning particularly as the Fed hikes rates. The repositioning stops once demand
rises again when yields reach attractive levels for the more patient longer term
investment community. The supply shocks ultimately end and debt and deficit worries will
likely remain, but they still take a backseat for now. Longer duration government debt is

becoming more attractive each week even in the face of the concerns above. Be patient

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

S&P 500 Index Price Return is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States. A price return index is a stock
market index that tracks only the price changes and capital gains or losses of its component securities, without including dividends or cash distributions.
S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index is an early monthly economic indicator that estimates overall private-sector business activity by combining manufacturing

Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.
Nonfinancial assets, such as closely held businesses, real estate, fine art, oil, gas and mineral properties, and timber, farm and ranch land, are complex in nature and involve risks including total

Document 319: Could Midterms Give Wall Street A Cold Shower_

Subject: Could Midterms Give Wall Street A Cold Shower?

Could Midterms Give Wall Street A Cold Shower?

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Could Midterms Give Wall Street
A Cold Shower?
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 2 Nov 2026
View in browser

I. When Gridlock Isn't Good for Stocks
The US midterm elections will be over tomorrow night. According to
betting markets, Democrats have roughly a 95% probability of winning the
House and a 76% probability of taking the Senate (charts).
We suspect that stock investors have yet to fully discount the possibility of
a Democratic sweep of Congress. Some may remain skeptical that polls
confirming strong support for Democrats are accurate. They might reckon

### Page 2

that Democrats are more likely than Republicans to talk to pollsters over
the phone.
In any event, Wall Street traditionally welcomes political gridlock because
it reduces the likelihood that Congress will pass extremist legislation. But
this time could be different for two reasons.
First, deepening partisanship could turn gridlock into damaging political
paralysis at a time when the Bond Vigilantes are already uneasy about
Washington's unsustainable fiscal trajectory. A Democratic sweep could

complicate negotiations over government spending and the debt ceiling.
Prolonged shutdowns and fiscal standoffs could push Treasury yields
higher, weighing on equity valuations.

### Page 3

Second, gridlock's traditional ability to constrain policymaking may not
hold this time. President Donald Trump has relied heavily on executive
authority, with some of his most market-disruptive policies, including tariffs
and military action against Iran, pursued largely without congressional
approval. A Democratic-controlled Congress may therefore do little to
restrain him. Moreover, with the midterms behind him, Trump may feel
even less constrained politically and more willing to pursue controversial

policies through executive action. Hence, a blue wave could deliver a cold
shower for stocks, though we would view a significant selloff as a buying
opportunity given the remarkable strength of the economy and corporate
earnings.
One encouraging development ahead of the elections was today's reports
that the US and Iran are working on a peaceful diplomatic solution to their
war. Iranian President Masoud Pezeshkian said he has granted his
government greater authority to pursue peace talks, while President

Trump and Vice President Vance also signaled progress. Hopes for a deal
sent the price of Brent crude oil down 7.6% to $104.77 per barrel (chart).
However, we have been here before with apparent diplomatic
breakthroughs. We still see a high risk of military re-escalation. The bid-
ask spread remains wide, with Tehran demanding sanctions relief, the
release of frozen assets, and an end to the US naval blockade of Iranian
ports, while Washington insists on nuclear concessions. Meanwhile, Iran's

leverage over the Strait of Hormuz has been weakening. According to
shipping data, for roughly half of September, Gulf oil exporters shipped
more crude than they were shipping before the war.

### Page 4

II. US Economy Continues To Roar
The US economy remains strong despite the volatility of the domestic
political and geopolitical scenes. Consider the following:
(1) Construction spending. Construction spending fell 1.9% m/m in
September to $2.19 trillion (saar), reversing most of August's upwardly
revised 2.0% increase. Nevertheless, spending remains just below last
year's record high (chart).
September's decline was driven by a 5.5% m/m drop in private residential

construction, attributable to a 13.0% plunge in home improvements
(chart). Elevated materials costs and higher mortgage rates continue to
weigh on the housing sector. Meanwhile, private nonresidential
construction rose 1.1% m/m, supported by continued investment in data
centers and power infrastructure.

### Page 5

The AI investment boom remains a powerful tailwind. Data center
construction spending surged 8.6% m/m and 90% y/y in September,
reaching a record $96.5 billion annual rate (chart).
(2) Manufacturing. The ISM M-PMI rose from 54.5 in September to 55.5
in October, marking its tenth consecutive month of expansion, the longest
streak since 2022. Production jumped to the highest reading since
November 2021, while new orders edged up to 55.5. Employment also

remained in expansionary territory (chart).

### Page 6

Inflationary pressures remain troublesome. The prices-paid index eased
from 77.9 to 75.2 in October but remained above 70 for the ninth
consecutive month. Regional Fed surveys also point to persistent price
pressures. Higher energy costs, tariffs, and elevated metals prices
continue to boost input costs (chart).
(3) Bank lending. The Fed's latest Senior Loan Officer Opinion Survey
(SLOOS) painted a mixed picture of credit demand. Banks reported
weaker demand for commercial and industrial (C&I) loans, mortgages,

and auto loans in Q4, while credit-card demand strengthened (chart).

### Page 7

Lending standards remained broadly unchanged for C&I loans but
tightened somewhat for commercial real estate loans (chart).
Encouragingly, actual bank lending tells a happier story. C&I loans surged
11.3% y/y through October 21, while total loans and leases increased
7.9% y/y (chart).

### Page 8

Credit growth is broad across bank sizes, with lending up 8.1% y/y at
large banks and 5.4% at small banks (chart).
Meanwhile, banks' allowances for loan and lease losses have remained
relatively stable at around $202 billion, despite the acceleration in lending
(chart).

### Page 9

clause.

ADVANCED ECONOMIES
In brief. The week was dominated by a continued global selloff in tech
stocks, leading to safe haven flows into US Treasuries, Japanese Go -
vernment bonds and German bunds. As a result, their yields fell des -
pite energy prices surging to or past their Spring highs. Economic data
generally showed continued resilience. French bonds were under rising

pressure, but Moody's unexpected decision to maintain its France ra -
ting unchanged could, at the margin, help stabilise the spread. The Fed,
ECB and BOJ should be on hold next week while signaling further rate
action is likely.
EUROPEAN UNION
A win for securitization. The Council and the European Parliament have
reached a provisional political agreement on measures to revive and
simplify the EU’s securitization market. The updated framework will

reduce banks’ capital requirements for the safest and most well-struc-
tured transactions, notably for a newly introduced “resilient” category
of securitization. As a key component of the “One Europe, One Market”
initiative and the EU’s Savings and Investment Union agenda, this aims
to channel increased lending to households and businesses. The rele -
vant legal texts must now be finalised.
EUROZONE
Expansion continues. The flash PMIs weakened in October, mainly on

the back of a decline in France. The EZ composite index dipped to 51.9
(-1.2 m/m), dragged down by services (51.7, -1.3 m/m), although ma -
nufacturing also dipped (52.4, -0.5 m/m). Both remain in expansion
territory, and manufacturing new export orders rose to their highest
level since early 2022. Price signals were also rather reassuring, with
the services output price index easing to 54.9 (-1.7 m/m). Households
are more cautious: consumer confidence fell for a second consecutive

month to a five-month low (-17.9). Households’ inflation expectations
edged up at short horizons (+0.1 pp to 3.6% at one year and 3.1% at
three years), but long-term expectations remained anchored, with the
five-year median stable at 2.5%.
France
Rising market stress despite decent economic news; a reprieve from
Moody’s. The spread on French 10-year bond relative to Germany’s
peaked to 174 bps on Friday, the highest since 2011 and only 16 bps
short of its all-time high, as markets price in anticipation of worst-case

scenarios regarding the country’s ability to repair its public finances.
The 10Y yield briefly exceeded 5.1% before falling back to 5% (above
the US equivalent). Nonetheless, services output was stable in August,
following a 0.6% m/m increase in July, a much higher pace than in Q2.
The rebound is driven by the catering sector, the digital sector, business
services, and real estate. The INSEE business climate improved from
96 to 97 (long-term average = 100) in October, but the survey was

conducted before the riots. The main improvement was in manufactu-
ring, which rose from 101 to 105, driven by increased new orders in the
aeronautics sector, and to a lesser extent by the services sector (from
98 to 99). The PMI survey (conducted partially after the riots) was
less positive: the composite index fell to 48.4 (-2.7 m/m), with a sharp
decline in services (48.4; -2.8 m/m) and a slightly smaller decline in
manufacturing (49.3; -1.3 m/m). Moody's unexpectedly announced af -

ter the market close on Friday that it was maintaining France’s ra -
ting at Aa3 on account of France’s diversified economic structure and
sound public institutions, but maintained its negative outlook. This is
on par with the UK and one notch above S&P and Fitch. Moody’s made
clear keeping this rating is conditional on a budget being adopted that
reduces the fiscal deficit, along with an easing of social and market
stress going forward. Saturday’s demonstrations across the country

saw strong attendance but only limited violent incidents.
Germany
Prices continue to rise, but confidence surveys remain on an upward
trend. Producer prices rose 6.7% y/y in September (+2.1pp m/m; the
highest level in 3.5 years) due to higher prices for energy and inter -
mediate goods, primarily metals. The ZEW Economic Sentiment Index
improved beyond consensus forecasts in October and returned to its
highest level since May 2023 (-35.9; +11.2 m/m), albeit the Economic

Expectations Index fell to its lowest level since July (28.1; -6.6 m/m).
The recovery in economic activity continues, although a slowdown is
evident: the flash Composite PMI slowed (52.8; -1 m/m; the lowest in
2 months) due to weaker growth in the services sector (51.7; -1.1 m/m;
the lowest in 2 months) and in manufacturing output (55.1; -0.9 m/m;
the lowest in 3 months). Nevertheless, in both sectors, hiring is rising
slightly, supported by growth in export sales and the spillover effects of

investment plans. Despite persistently high input prices, selling prices
are slowing (reaching their lowest level since February in the manu -
facturing sector).
UNITED KINGDOM
Inflation, wage growth and activity accelerate. CPI inflation overshot
consensus on energy prices, printing at 0.4% m/m and 3.5% y/y in Sep-
tember (consensus: 3.4%, prior: 3.1%), its highest in 11 months. Core
CPI came in at 2.8% y/y (up 0.2 pp), in line with consensus. Pressures

remained persistent according to the PPI at 8.3% y/y (prior: 6.7%). La -
bour market and activity data also surprised positively. Payroll growth
reached 102k in August (3m/3m), bringing the unemployment rate to
4.7% (down 0.2 pp), its lowest since July 2025. October PMI surveys
beat expectations in both manufacturing (52.8, consensus: 51.8, prior:
51.9) and services (53.1, consensus: 51.8, prior: 52.1). The only negative
news came from headline retail sales, falling 0.4% m/m in September,

with the core gauge (excluding petrol) deteriorating by -0.7% m/m.
UNITED STATES
Construction struggling, GENIUS Act implemented. Housing starts
dropped -5.2% m/m in September (-7.4% y/y). This third consecutive
monthly decline dragged the Q3 average to its lowest since Q2 2020.
Building permits also came in negative, at -0.2% m/m (-3.0% y/y), while
the NAHB homebuilder sentiment index plunged to its weakest since
2012. The average 30-year fixed mortgage rate eased to 7.32% (still

134bp above pre-war level), as the ongoing AI selloff pushed down
US treasury yields. The GENIUS Act final implementation rule from the
Office of the Comptroller of the Currency’s (OCC) leaves the door open
for stablecoin customers to receive yield or rewards. Issuers remain
prohibited from paying holders any form of interest or yield solely
in connection with the holding, use, or retention of payment stable -
coins. This represents a shift from the OCC’s February proposal and

has renewed concerns within the banking industry about deposit flight.
The rule will take effect on January 18, 2027.
JAPAN
Activity and inflation keep rising. Composite PMI was unchanged in
September at 52.3, solidly in expansion territory, as an improvement
in services activity offset slower output growth in manufacturing. The
Manufacturing PMI disappointed at 53.3 (-0.8 m/m; an 8-month low).
Underlying price pressures, meanwhile, extended. Headline CPI was

### Page 2

flat on a monthly basis, bringing the annual rate up to 2.1% (up 0.4 pp).
Core CPI (excl. fresh food) matched it at 2.1% y/y (up 0.4 pp), in line
with consensus. More importantly, the New Core index, which strips
out fresh food and energy, rose sharply to 2.4% y/y (up 0.5 pp), its
fastest pace since January.
EMERGING ECONOMIES

ASIA
China
New monetary policy tool and export controls. The central bank’s Go-
vernor Pan Gongsheng unveiled a plan to switch (“when appropriate”)
its key policy rate from the 7-day reverse repo rate (at 1.4% since May
2025) to the overnight reverse repo rate, in order to reflect develop -
ments in the interbank market. New export controls: The government
plans to add 40 key technologies to the list of technologies prohibited
or restricted for export, including in the AI (for ex. LLMs, computing

clusters), biotechnology and photovoltaic sectors.
Indonesia
BI kept policy rate unchanged at 5.75%, raised its deposit facility rate
by 25bp to 5% and announced a phased cut in reserve requirements
from 9% to 7% by December. This combination of limited FX support
and measures to support credit growth sends mixed signals amidst
pressures on the rupiah following MSCI’s downgrade warning and un-
dermines the central bank’s credibility. The rupiah has lost 1% against

the USD since mid-October, with little contagion effect on other Asian
currencies.
South Korea
The Bank of Korea (BoK) kept its policy rate unchanged at 3.0%, but
the tightening cycle may not be over. CPI inflation was 2.9% y/y in Sep-
tember, above the central bank’s target of 2%, while high oil prices,
rapid growth in Seoul housing prices and wage pressures (though
concentrated in the electronics sector) raise the risk of lasting infla -
tion pressure. As economic activity remains robust, supported by strong

exports and recovering investment, the BoK has scope to raise rates
further.
EMEA
Hungary
Monetary status quo and 2027 budget in consolidation mode. The
Central Bank kept its policy rate unchanged at 5.5%, as expected. A cau-
tious stance will likely prevail in the short term. Inflation has been
picking up in recent months, albeit mildly, and will continue to move
upward as the government recently removed the margin cap on fuel.
Separately, the Budget for 2027 and the medium-term fiscal plan show

Hungary’s goal to join the Eurozone; the government expects to reduce
the fiscal deficit to 4.9% of GDP in 2027 (from 7% expected in 2026) and
towards 2.8% of GDP by 2030.
Türkiye
Cautious monetary loosening. The Central Bank lowered its policy rate
by 100 bp to 36% at its October Monetary Policy Committee meeting,
in line with market expectations. The overnight lending and borrowing
rates were also reduced by 100 bp, to 39% and 34.5%, respectively.

The improvement in underlying inflation has yet to translate into a
sustained period of low inflation, justifying the CBRT’s preference for a
measured pace of monetary easing.
LATIN AMERICA
Brazil
President Lula da Silva was re-elected on Sunday for a fourth term
(with 51.8% of the vote), defeating far right-wing candidate Flavio Bol-
sonaro (48.2%). Lula will enter this new term facing significant econo -
mic and political challenges. Public debt has risen to more than 82% of

GDP , while high borrowing costs and rigid mandatory spending leave
limited room to reconcile fiscal consolidation with social spending and
investment. Restoring confidence in the fiscal outlook will be a key
challenge in the short term.
COMMODITIES
Pressure rising. Amid growing rumours of imminent re-escalation of
military operations in the Gulf, energy prices rose sharply this week,
with brent touching $117/b – near its April peak, and European gas

prices (TTF) rising to 97 €/MWh on Monday, their highest level since
Dec 2022. The Asian gas benchmark (JKM) is following the same trend
(4.6% w/w to 31.4 $/mbtu, equivalent to around 96 €/MWh). Diesel
crack spreads are slightly decreasing in Europe (-2.8% w/w on Monday)
and in the US (-4% w/w on Monday). The first Russian diesel tanker
intended to the US market left Russia this Friday and should supply the
US market in 2 weeks. US diesel average retail prices remain oriented

to the upside (0.7% w/w at 6.32 $/gallon on Monday). US commercial
crude inventories fell by 3.1 mb in the week ending 16 October, signi -
ficantly exceeding market expectations for a 340,000-barrel decline.

Document 352: Germany _ An export-driven recovery (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Germany _ An export-driven recovery (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

10
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
AN EXPORT-DRIVEN RECOVERY
Growth, which began in the fourth quarter of 2025, gained momentum in the first half of 2026. This momentum should
continue, with expected growth of 1.1% in 2026 and 1.3% in 2027. In the short term, the main driver will still be exports,
particularly to Europe, which will contribute to restructuring industry. The impact of investment plans is expected to

strengthen in 2027, while domestic demand is likely to remain constrained by inflation (2.7% in 2026 and 2.6 % in 2027),
which will weigh on purchasing power. Fiscal stimulus and structural reforms are expected to continue to support growth.
However, the rise in the deficit and debt levels means that the increase in long-term interest rates observed so far is mostly
structural.
A RETURN TO GROWTH DRIVEN BY EXTERNAL TRADE
The first half of 2026 confirms the rebound in growth (0.4% and then

0.3% q/q in Q1 and Q2). This growth is driven by foreign trade (contribu-
tions from net exports of +0.8 pp and +0.2 pp, respectively, with +0.9 pp
on average coming from exports and -0.4 pp from imports), following
two years of contracting exports, particularly those to Europe (see be -
low). The increase in public expenditure ( see our analysis ) reflects
the implementation of investment plans, with the multiplier effect on
growth of these plans expected to increase.

In the second half of the year, growth is expected to remain robust,
pushing the annual average to 1.1% in 2026 (following 0.3% in 2025).
Exports are set to remain the main driver, thanks to continued strong
European demand, which is compensating for domestic demand that
remains sluggish. Growth of private consumption will, in fact, remain
subdued by energy inflation. Business investment is expected to be
driven by dynamic sectors (defence, technology equipment and aeros -

pace), which would limit the impact of rising interest rates.
In 2027, growth (1.3%) would be based more heavily on domestic de -
mand, with greater spillover effects from investment plans and a likely
recovery in household consumption (following a peak in inflation in
Q1 2027). At the same time, external demand is expected to remain
strong, but its contribution to growth would be lower than in 2026.
This is because the stronger growth in domestic demand would stimu-

late imports to a greater extent.
INDUSTRY IS BENEFITING FROM AI INVESTMENT AND INTRA-EUROPEAN
MOMENTUM
The business climate in industry improved significantly at the start of
the second half of the year (the manufacturing PMI has risen by 4 points
since May, reaching a four-year high in August). New export orders have
even reached their highest level since early 2022. These positive signs
put into perspective the risks associated with the summer’s climate

events (heatwaves and the historic drought on the Rhine), which dis -
rupted industrial activity and river transport.
Since early 2025, output in some sectors has rebounded, particularly in
the electronics sector (+6.4 %). Manufacturers of electrical equipment,
as well as IT and electronics firms, are benefiting in particular from
the wave of investment in data centres and defence. If this momentum
continues, it could offset some of the losses recorded in the automotive

and chemicals sectors, which have both seen the sharpest falls in out-
put since late 2017 (-12.8% and -19.4%, respectively).
The sustained increase in military spending on medical technologies
and robotics across Europe is driving a restructuring of the interme -
diate-goods sector. Companies exposed to struggling sectors are gra -
dually turning towards these new markets. However, this reorientation
is not fully offsetting the loss of historical markets.

The rebound in German industry is also being fuelled by European
demand. While exports to China (-12.7% YTD in July) and the United
States (-1.5%) have certainly continued to fall, exports to a number of
European Union countries have risen sharply, particularly in Southern
and Central Europe. This shift is benefiting machinery and transport
equipment (47.6% of goods exports, up 20.5% since January 2025) in
particular. We expect this momentum to continue in 2027, creating a

virtuous cycle that reinforces European growth.
A STAGNANT LABOUR MARKET
The situation in the German labour market has stabilised over the
past several quarters, but it is not yet showing signs of improvement.
The unemployment rate is shifting relatively little (4% in November;
+0.2 pp y/y) and remains below the Eurozone average (6.4%). Falling
employment in the manufacturing sector (-0.6% since Q4 2019) is offset
by an increase in the services sector (+0.9%). Nevertheless, the latest

business climate surveys suggest that the situation may stabilise. The
gradual retraining of the workforce in struggling sectors is helping to
meet some of the needs arising from new investment.
Generally speaking, in the longer term, pension reform should support
the labour supply. As a matter of fact, it will help to increase the num-
ber of workers gradually, notably through the phasing out of early reti-
rement at 63 and the indexing of the retirement age to life expectancy

from 2032 onwards.
GERMANY
Growth and inflation

world
Although the unemployment rate is set to remain low, wage growth
would remain limited, particularly in 2026, as negotiated wages will
offset inflation to a lesser extent than they will in 2027. The planned
increases in the minimum wage (+8% in 2026 and +5% in 2027, to
EUR 14.6 per hour), significant as they are, will have little knock-on

effect on the rest of the wage scale.
PURCHASING POWER CONTINUES TO BE SQUEEZED BY HIGH INFLATION
Wage purchasing power is expected to fall further in 2026, having
already lost 4 pp since 2022, before stabilising in 2027. The reduction
in income tax (by EUR 10 billion, or 0.2% of GDP), which will come into
force next January, is also expected to support the disposable income of
low- and middle-income households.
Inflation has been rising again since the start of the summer and is

expected to remain above 2% throughout our forecast horizon. It has
accelerated since June (+0.5 pp to +2.9% y/yin August) driven by a fur -
ther rise in energy prices (+6.7 pp to +9.4% y/y), while core inflation re-
mains stable at +2.6%. From spring 2027 onwards, the expected easing
in energy prices (particularly gas prices) should bring down inflation.
However, this decline will be limited by the inflationary effects of the
wider roll-out of investment plans, as well as by the expected rise in

food prices.
This inflation, which has been structurally higher since the onset of the
2022 energy shock, will weigh on household consumption. Furthermore,
German households’ propensity to save remains high by international
standards (with a net balance 15 points higher on average over the
last ten years, according to the European Commission), which is also
weighing on their consumption.
FISCAL STIMULUS CONTINUES
After recording a public deficit of -3% of GDP in 2025, we anticipate a

further deterioration in the public balance to -4% in 2026, then -4.2%
in 2027. The primary deficit is projected to reach -2.8% of GDP in 2026
(after -1.9% in 2025) before narrowing slightly to -2.7% in 2027. At the
same time, the interest burden is expected to rise gradually from 1.1%
of GDP in 2025 to 1.5% in 2027. This combination will result in a rapid
rise in the debt-to-GDP ratio, from 62.6% of GDP in 2025 to 66.1% in
2026 and then 69.7% in 2027, before approaching 85% by the end of the

decade.
The yield on the 10-year Bund rose above 3.5% on 14 September, its
highest level since 2007. In addition to the increase in issues linked to
extra-budgetary funds, inflationary pressures are also contributing to
this rise. A gradual easing of inflation next year would make a mode -
rate easing of sovereign yields possible in 2027. However, yields would
remain high, at 3.2% in Q4 2027, suggesting that the bulk of the rise is
structural.
At the same time, the government is continuing to implement its pro -

gramme of structural reforms, including measures to support business
investment, health-insurance reform, pension reform and an overhaul
of the tax system. A number of measures have come into force, par -
ticularly accelerated depreciation for business investment until 2027,
which will be followed by a reduction in corporation tax from 2028.
Administrative simplification is also set to lead to an overhaul of the
tax system from January 2027 1. The health-insurance reform has been

passed, while the pension reform is still under discussion, with expecta-
tions that it will be adopted by the end of the year.
1  The main measure will be a reduction of EUR 10 billion per year in income tax for individuals on low incomes, funded in part by an increase in taxes on high earners. The bill will
be submitted to Parliament in the autumn, before being implemented from 2027.
Article completed on 14 September 2026
Lucie Barette
lucie.barette@bnpparibas.com

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
FIJI ECONOMIC UPDATE
Fiji’s resilience is being tested...
Fiji’s economy entered 2026 with growth already normalising
from the post-pandemic recovery. Real GDP growth slowed from
3.6% in 2024 to 2.5% in 2025. Foreign reserves started the year

around $3.7bn, banking-system liquidity remained high and the
RBF maintained its Overnight Policy Rate at 0.25%.
Activity so far into 2026 has been somewhat stronger than what
we feared. Visitor arrivals reached 762,352 during January–
September, up 3.7%yr, while construction, investment lending,
cement sales, remittances and private-sector credit remained
firm. Nevertheless, Westpac Fiji has kept its 2026 growth
forecast unchanged at 2.0%. Stronger headline indicators are

not translating one-for-one into real economic growth because
higher prices and intermediate costs are absorbing a growing
share of nominal activity.
The outlook changed materially in March as the Middle East
conflict disrupted global energy markets. Fiji remains heavily
dependent on imported petroleum products, so higher refined-
fuel, freight and insurance costs flowed through transport,
electricity, food distribution and wider business expenses. By

June, motor spirit price reached $3.93/litre and diesel at a record
$4.58/litre, while automotive-fuel sales value rose 24.4%yr in the
June quarter.
As this report goes out, the Middle East conflict remains a
key external downside risk. At least 12 vessels were attacked
in the week ending 16 October and yet it is reported that oil
flows through Hormuz averaged 11.6mn barrels per day to 13
October, about 68% of pre-war levels through alternative and

costly routes. Iran says a seven-day reopening proposal remains
available, but US-Iran negotiations remain unresolved.
Westpac Economics’ most recent scenario analysis “ Persistent
energy pressures have further to run ” indicates that relief will
be gradual. Brent crude is forecast to average US$101/bbl in Q4
2026 before easing through 2027. Refined products are expected
to normalise more slowly because refinery capacity, inventories,
freight and insurance remain constrained. Under Westpac’s

escalation scenario, in a return to open conflict Brent could reach
around US$150/bbl in H1 2027.
Domestic tourism sector demonstrates the nominal-versus-
real growth tension. June-quarter visitor arrivals increased to
287,588, yet earnings fell 4.9%yr to $708.1mn. First-half tourism
earnings declined 1.7%yr despite higher arrivals, even though
hotel takings increased 9.5%yr.
Australia and New Zealand remain critical, together contributing
around 71% of September arrivals. Westpac expects Australian

growth to slow from 2.1% in 2026 to 1.5% in 2027, while New
Zealand strengthens from 1.9% to 2.6%. Tourism therefore
increasingly needs to focus on yield rather than visitor numbers
alone.
Shamal Chand
Senior Economist, Westpac Pacific Economics

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
FIJI ECONOMIC UPDATE
...but the buffers are still doing the heavy lifting...
There are good reasons why growth held up despite these shocks.
Construction work put in place reached $155.9mn in the June
quarter, up 8.6%yr, while investment lending, cement sales and

construction-related imports subsequently remained strong.
Industrial production increased 1.5%yr, although performance
remained uneven across industries.
Inward personal remittances have been particularly impressive,
reaching $1.2bn during January–August, up 32.7%yr. We expect
remittances to reach $1.55bn this year. These inflows, with strong
diaspora support, are helping households absorb higher living
costs while simultaneously supporting Fiji’s external position.

However, the external accounts show the cost of this resilience.
Fiji’s merchandise trade deficit widened to $2.9bn in the first half,
with imports reaching $4.3bn against exports of $1.4bn. Mineral-
fuel imports alone reached around $1.2bn, up 57%yr. Foreign
reserves nevertheless recovered to approximately $4.09bn by
end-September, equivalent to 5.7 months of retained imports , also
supported by Government’s external loan draw-down.
Our in-house import-demand indicator is signalling softer

domestic momentum. Import activity over the three months
to September was down 21.3%yr and fell 39.8% compared
with the preceding three months. The broader six-month
measure declined at a more moderate 16.1% annualised pace.
While volatile, the trend adds to our view that higher prices are
weighing on real activity, despite continued strength across
tourism, remittances, credit and construction.
Headline inflation reached 7.6%yr in August before easing to

6.8% in September, with transport prices up 12.2%yr and food
prices up 7.6%. Our latest forecast has November inflation
around 8.0%yr, average inflation around 4.3% for 2026 and
December (year-end) inflation at 7.4%.
Higher fuel prices are no longer affecting only households.
The shock is moving through freight, electricity, agriculture,
construction materials and wider supply chains. For instance,
we note recent increases in taxi and bus fares. These higher

intermediate costs compress business margins and reduce
household purchasing power.
This also informs our 2027 view. Westpac Fiji has downgraded
its 2027 growth forecast to 2.5%, reflecting the prevailing
effects of the energy crisis, elevated refined-fuel prices and the
broader transmission of higher intermediate costs into next year.
Westpac’s global outlook supports this caution: crude prices are
expected to ease gradually, but refined products could remain

comparatively expensive for longer.
Renewable energy is increasingly important because the fuel
crisis has put a clear economic price on Fiji’s imported-energy
dependence. The faster Fiji prioritises a shift to renewable energy,
the sooner it can establish a more stable energy foundation.
El Niño creates an additional risk through agriculture, hydro
generation and food prices. The buffers remain meaningful, but
they are being asked to work significantly harder.

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
FIJI ECONOMIC UPDATE
...as risks converge
Government finances add another dimension to the outlook.
The FY2025/26 deficit came in at $597.9mn, or 4.3% of GDP,
substantially better than originally budgeted.
Nevertheless, financing requirements are substantial.

Government plans to raise $1.48bn in FY2026/27, comprising
$1.046bn in deficit financing and $436.3mn refinancing. Around
$853.9mn to be domestically sourced and $627.95mn externally,
including a $444mn ADB policy-based loan in November.
Domestic yields therefore deserve closer attention. Fiji’s
government yield curve has steepened considerably, with the
adjustment concentrated beyond 10 years. While the 10-year
yield remained at 3.90%, the 20-year yield rose by 80 basis points

between July 2025 and September 2026 to 5.80%. The 25-year
yield increased even more sharply, climbing from 5.94% to 7.19%
after peaking at 7.25% in mid-2026.
Pressure is also emerging at the short end. The Government’s
20 October Treasury Bill tender offered $50mn across three-,
six- and 12-month maturities. Investors bid for the full amount,
but sought yields of 0.35%, 0.60% and 1.30%, respectively,
above the listed yields of 0.30%, 0.55% and 1.25%. Government

did not accept the higher yields, leaving the entire $50mn offer
unallocated. This suggests investors are increasingly seeking
better returns even on shorter-dated Government securities.
The financial system still provides considerable protection.
Banking-system liquidity stood around $2.28bn at end-
September and the RBF maintained its OPR at 0.25%. In August,
the weighted-average lending rate was 4.51%, the savings-
deposit rate 0.31% and the time-deposit rate 2.01%.

For now, high liquidity limits immediate crowding-out risks.
Higher government yields may encourage banks and institutional
investors to allocate more funds towards government securities.
If liquidity subsequently tightens, deposit rates may need to rise
to retain savings, adding pressure to bank funding costs and
eventually lending rates.
S&P’s decision to maintain Fiji’s B+/B ratings but revise the
outlook from stable to negative reinforces the importance of

debt sustainability, investor confidence and credible medium-
term fiscal consolidation.
Political uncertainty adds another layer, with ongoing public
debate on constitutional amendments and the general election
due by 6 February 2027.
This brings us back to the growth story. We have maintained
2026 growth at 2.0% because resilient tourism, remittances,
construction and investment are being offset by unexpectedly
high inflation and intermediate costs. Our downgrade of 2027

growth to 2.5% recognises that this adjustment will take longer
than previously expected, while fiscal, financial, energy, climate
and political risks increasingly overlap.

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
INFLATION OUTLOOK
Cost pressures move beyond the pump
Inflation has emerged as a key pressure point for Fiji, threatening 
to erode gains from otherwise resilient domestic activity. 
Headline inflation accelerated to 6.8%yr in September, driven by 
essentials: food prices rose 7.6%yr, transport increased 12.2%yr 
and housing and utilities were 4.9% higher. The underlying 
picture remains less severe, with prices declining 0.4%mth in 
September and annual-average inflation at 1.9%. Nevertheless, 
the acceleration suggests the global fuel shock is increasingly 
working its way through the domestic economy.

The import-price pipeline reinforces this view. Import prices 
increased 4.9%qtr and 4.4%yr in the June quarter, including 
a 15.4%yr rise in mineral-product prices as petrol, diesel and 
aviation-fuel costs increased. Export prices rose 6.2%yr, although 
the terms of trade eased 0.6%qtr. Elevated energy costs are 
also spreading beyond the pump. Transport-services producer 
prices increased 2.7%qtr in June, while building-material prices 
rose 1.5%qtr in September, signalling broader pressure across 
distribution, construction and services.

Fuel-price pressures intensified again in October, when regulated 
unleaded prices in Viti Levu increased to $3.15/litre and diesel 
rose by 21 cents to $3.41/litre. Prices nevertheless remain below 
their June peaks. Our indicative estimates point to further 
increases in fuel prices over the coming months.

The global outlook offers little immediate relief. Westpac 
Economics expects Brent to average US$101/bbl in Q4 2026 
before easing to US$77/bbl by Q4 2027, but refined fuels are 
expected to ease more slowly as refinery supply remains more 
constrained, while shipping, insurance and inventory pressures 
gradually normalise. Westpac’s Australian forecasts provide 
some regional context, with diesel and petrol expected to 
average 274c/l and 230c/l, respectively, in Q4 before declining 
through 2027.

For Fiji, the distinction between crude and refined-product prices 
is important. Singapore gasoil prices, freight and insurance costs 
are expected to remain elevated and ease only gradually through 
2027, meaning lower Brent prices may not translate immediately 
into cheaper imported fuel. Higher diesel costs feed into freight, 
construction, agriculture and distribution, while aviation-
fuel pressures add to airline operating costs and tourism-
sector margins. Repeated shocks increase the likelihood that 
businesses eventually pass more of these costs on to consumers, 
particularly through food and other essentials.

We consequently expect inflation to remain elevated through 
year-end, rising towards 8.0% in November before easing to 7.4% 
in December, taking annual-average inflation to around 4.3%. 
The concern is increasingly that an imported fuel shock develops 
into broader cost-push inflation into next year. The squeeze on 
real purchasing power makes inflation an increasingly important 
downside risk to Fiji’s growth outlook heading into 2027.
Source: FBoS, Macrobond, Westpac Economics

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
CREDIT RATING OUTLOOK
S&P negative outlook is concerning
Fiji’s sovereign credit profile has come under renewed pressure
recently, adding another layer of caution to an economy already
navigating softer real growth and elevated external risks.

S&P maintained Fiji’s long-term rating at B+ and short-term
rating at B but revised the outlook from Stable to Negative.
While not a downgrade, it signals increased risk if fiscal
and debt metrics deteriorate. Historically, four of Fiji’s six
negative outlooks since 2006 have resulted in downgrades,
typically during periods of major economic or fiscal stress.
Similar vulnerabilities are resurfacing, with rising government
expenditure and guarantees, alongside Middle East tensions and

inflationary pressures.
The key concern is fiscal. S&P viewed the 2026/27 Budget as
more expansionary than previously expected, effectively slowing
the fiscal consolidation achieved in previous years. It projects
the fiscal deficit to average 4.6% of GDP over 2027–29, including
4.9% in FY2027, with net government debt moving above 70% of
GDP. Once contingent liabilities are incorporated, the broader
public-sector exposure approaches 80% of GDP. This leaves

considerably less fiscal space to absorb another major domestic
or external shock.
In S&P’s latest assessment, contingent liabilities are becoming
increasingly important to the sovereign risk story. Government
exposure through guarantees and other contingent liabilities
adds to the broader fiscal risk, while a widening current account
deficit, elevated imports and rising external debt reinforce the
risks surrounding the outlook. For Fiji, this matters because

the next phase of growth will require substantial investment in
infrastructure, climate resilience and productive capacity.
The negative outlook therefore strengthens the case for a more
credible medium-term fiscal consolidation path by the Fiji
Government. S&P indicated that the outlook could return to
Stable if economic growth and tourism outperform its forecasts,
resulting in narrower fiscal deficits and lower debt ratios, or if
improvements in institutional and policy settings sustainably

strengthen fiscal outcomes and debt levels.
Importantly, the assessment across rating agencies is not
uniformly negative. Moody’s maintained Fiji’s B1 long-term issuer
rating with a Stable outlook, suggesting that despite significant
structural and fiscal challenges, the overall credit profile remains
broadly unchanged and risks remain balanced.
A future downgrade would likely reinforce perceptions of
higher sovereign risk just as Fiji requires greater private and

foreign investment. Maintaining fiscal credibility, containing
contingent liabilities and ensuring that new borrowing supports
productivity-enhancing investment will be increasingly
important. With elevated uncertainty, climate vulnerability and
external shocks remaining additional downside risks, the room
for further fiscal slippage is becoming increasingly limited for the
Fijian Government.

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
SUPER EL NIÑO
The threat of a “very strong” El Niño
It is the year of the ‘very strong’ El Niño, and for Fiji the risk is no
longer just on the horizon. Dry conditions are already affecting
parts of the country, while the Government has stepped up

preparations for a potentially more difficult period ahead.
History suggests this matters for the broader economy because
prolonged drought can simultaneously affect agriculture, water
supply and electricity generation before flowing through food
prices, imports, rural incomes and ultimately growth.
Our rainfall analysis covering 1981–2025 shows that rainfall
during El Niño periods was 24.7% lower than under neutral
conditions nationally, with all four regions recording historical

deficits above 20%.
During the 1982–83 El Niño, severe drought coincided with
Cyclone Oscar and a weak global economy. Historical estimates
highlight that Fiji’s economy contracted by 4.0%, against a
pre-disaster forecast of 2.7% growth during the super El Niño
period then. The 1997–98 episode was more severe. By 1998 the
cane crop was forecast at just 1.88mn tonnes, producing around
150,000 tonnes of sugar. Historical estimates used here indicate

cane production fell around 26%, contributing to an aggregate
GDP contraction of at least 1.3%. Fiji also devalued the dollar by
20% in January 1998 amid the overlapping Asian Financial Crisis.
The social cost was significant. In 1998, around 27,562
households needed food relief and 47,592 were already receiving
emergency water supplies according to some surveys. The 2015–
16 episode again brought crop losses and drying water supplies.
The effects are beginning to show

The current event is already leaving a footprint. September
rainfall was drier than usual across the Western, Central and
Eastern divisions and most of the Northern Division, while several
Western Division stations recorded less than 100mm.
More recent monitoring points to serious or very dry conditions
across parts of Vanua Levu, Taveuni, Lomaiviti, Yasawa, Lau and
other areas. Local effects are becoming tangible. Taveuni had
already required assistance with water carting in September,

while NDRMO reported that Fiji was experiencing meteorological
drought and warned it could progress towards hydrological and
agricultural drought if conditions continued.
The Government’s response has consequently shifted towards
preparedness. The National Disaster Risk Management Council
is reviewing the National El Niño Preparedness and Response
Plan. Humanitarian clusters were also instructed to prepare
contingency plans for consolidation and presentation to Cabinet,

while NDRMO has been working with Energy Fiji Limited and the
Water Authority of Fiji to assess emerging risks.
This marks a progression from earlier in the year. In August,
our assessment was that the FY2026–27 Budget contained
no explicit El Niño preparedness measure, implying much
of the initial response would need to occur through existing
institutional arrangements.
The economic transmission remains important. Agriculture feels
reduced rainfall first through weaker soil moisture and water

availability. Lower domestic food production can then lift prices
and import requirements. Electricity provides another channel
as weaker hydro generation could require increased thermal
generation and imported fuel, adding to higher production
costs and foreign-exchange outflows. And Fiji has less room for
another supply-side shock.
Fiji is more diversified than during the droughts of the 1980s
and 1990s, while its reserve position provides an important

buffer. But financial buffers cannot replace rainfall, agricultural
production or water supplies.

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
RESEARCH INSIGHTS
Why is ‘foreign reserves’ so important for Fiji?
Foreign reserves remain one of the most closely watched indica -
tors of Fiji’s economic health. At the end of September, reserves 
stood at around $4.1 billion, equivalent to 5.7 months of retained 
imports. The figure features prominently in Reserve Bank report -
ing, and for good reason. 
For a small, import-dependent economy operating a managed 
exchange-rate regime, reserves provide an important buffer 
against external shocks.
In simple terms, foreign reserves can be thought of as Fiji’s 
foreign-currency savings account. Fiji earns foreign exchange 
through tourism, remittances, exports, foreign investment and 
external borrowing. At the same time, foreign currency is required 
to pay for imports, service external debt and meet other overseas 
obligations. When foreign-currency inflows exceed outflows, 
reserves generally rise. When the reverse occurs, reserves come 
under pressure.

The analogy is not perfect, but it highlights why reserves matter. 
Unlike some larger economies, Fiji cannot simply rely on its ex -
change rate to absorb external shocks. The Fiji dollar is managed 
against a basket comprising the Australian, US and New Zealand 
dollars, the euro and the Japanese yen. This provides a degree 
of exchange-rate stability and predictability for households and 
businesses, but it also means that foreign reserves are needed to 
effectively provide a shock absorber for the economy.

There is also an important connection with domestic financial 
conditions. Our analysis suggests that before July 2020, every 
$1 million increase in reserves was associated with around $0.31 
million in additional banking-system liquidity. Since then, the 
relationship has strengthened substantially to around $0.83 mil -
lion. Following the pandemic, external borrowing, grants, remit -
tances and the recovery in tourism helped lift foreign-exchange 
inflows and thus banking liquidity, while government expenditure 
and limited sterilisation further contributed to abundant domes -
tic liquidity.

This highlights an important policy trade-off. Economists refer to 
the “impossible trinity”, where an economy cannot simultaneous -
ly maintain a fixed or tightly managed exchange rate, completely 
free capital movement and fully independent monetary policy. 
Fiji’s framework therefore relies on a combination of foreign re -
serves, monetary-policy settings, exchange controls and broader 
market management to maintain stability.

Those controls, however, are not without cost. Restrictions on 
moving capital offshore limit the flexibility available to house -
holds, foreign investors and Fiji-based businesses. 
A deeper question is therefore whether Fiji could eventually move 
towards a more flexible exchange-rate framework.

Probably, but not yet. Greater exchange-rate flexibility could 
theoretically allow the currency itself to absorb more of an 
external shock. But that requires considerably deeper financial 
markets than what Fiji presently has. Foreign-exchange trading 
remains relatively shallow, secondary-market activity in govern -
ment securities is limited, while instruments that allow business -
es to hedge currency risk are significantly underdeveloped.

There is also an important transmission issue. A weaker Fiji dollar 
could theoretically improve export competitiveness, but the 
benefits would take time. Merchandise exports remain con -
strained by domestic production capacity, while tourism is also 
influenced by available capacity and advance bookings. Higher 
import prices, by contrast, would flow through much faster. Given 
Fiji’s heavy reliance on imported fuel, food, machinery and other 
goods, currency depreciation would quickly add to inflation and 
business costs.

The experiences of Australia and Singapore offer two useful per -
spectives.
 Australia floated its dollar in 1983 alongside broader finan -
cial-sector liberalisation and market development. Singapore 
followed a different path. Despite possessing a highly developed 
financial system, it continues to manage its currency against a 
basket, with the exchange rate occupying a central role in mone -
tary policy.

For Fiji, the lesson is not that one exchange-rate regime is nec -
essarily superior to another. Rather, greater flexibility requires 
deeper markets and stronger transmission mechanisms. Devel -
oping the foreign-exchange market, domestic capital markets 
and currency-risk management instruments therefore needs to 
precede any significant shift in the present regime.

Until then, foreign reserves will remain much more than another 
number in the RBF’s monthly statistics. It acts as a buffer against 
external shocks, help underpin confidence in the Fiji dollar and 
provide the economy with room to continue paying for essential 
imports when foreign-currency inflows weaken. At $4.1 billion, 
the reserve position remains comfortable, but maintaining that 
buffer remains fundamental to Fiji’s macroeconomic stability .

Document 471: “避风港”万亿市值狂飙过后，苹果能否支撑过财报考验？

Source PDF: all report pdf/“避风港”万亿市值狂飙过后，苹果能否支撑过财报考验？.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

苹果今夜将公布第四财季财报，市场预计营收同⽐增⻓ 11% ⾄ 1135 亿美元，净利润增⻓ 6%
⾄ 292 亿美元。折叠屏 iPhone Duo ⾸度纳⼊业绩观察，需求、供货及产品表现备受关注。
随着股价⼤涨、估值升⾄历史⾼位，财报能否兑现增⻓预期、延续强劲⾃由现⾦流，将成
为关键。
摘要：
苹果股价近⼏个⽉的强势上涨，令其在周⼀美股盘后财报公布前⼏乎没有任何容错空间。
⾃ 6 ⽉末低点以来，苹果股价累计上涨 25% ，市值增加约 1 万亿美元。在⾕歌和亚⻢逊等⼤型科
技同⾏⼤举投⼊ AI 基础设施、引发市场担忧之际，苹果稳健的营收增⻓与不断攀升的⾃由现⾦
流，使其成为投资者眼中的 " 避⻛港 " 。然⽽，这轮涨势也将苹果估值推⾄ 2008 年以来最⾼⽔平
—— 以未来 12 个⽉预期市盈率衡量，苹果达到 36 倍，约为标普 500 指数的两倍。
上周五，当⾕歌和亚⻢逊令⼈失望的财报拖累 AI 相关股票集体下挫、费城半导体指数单⽇重挫
7.2% 之际，苹果股价仅下跌 1.2% ，明显跑赢⼤盘，并借英伟达遭遇抛售之机，时隔数⽉重夺全

球市值最⾼公司宝座，市值达 5.03 万亿美元。本次财报将是对这⼀ " 避⻛港 " 叙事的⾸次真实压⼒
测试。
⾼估值之下，容错空间所剩⽆⼏
苹果此番上涨已使股价处于极度充分定价的状态，任何意外都可能引发估值重估。
Tandem Investment Advisors 联席⾸席投资官 Ben Carew 表⽰： "苹果当前估值处于历史 99% 分
位，当⼀只股票被如此充分定价时，⼀旦出现意外，⻛险便随之⽽来。⽬前它扮演着避险资产
的⻆⾊，但如果增⻓弱于市场预期，⼈们就会质疑它是否真的那么安全，届时估值倍数可能⾯
临重估。"
这种⻛险对于苹果的⻓期观察者⽽⾔并不陌⽣。三个⽉前，受零部件短缺拖累季度营收预期，
苹果股价单⽇跌幅创下 16 个⽉来最⼤。
华尔街预计，苹果第四财季营收将同⽐增⻓ 11% ⾄ 1135 亿美元，净利润增⻓ 6% ⾄ 292 亿美元。
若能达成，这将标志着苹果完成⾃ 2021 年以来最佳的全年销售增速 ——15% 。但据彭博汇编的
分析师平均预期，增速预计将在未来两年放缓。

⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
折叠屏 iPhone Duo 成关键变量
本次财报将⾸次披露苹果全新折叠屏机型 iPhone Duo 的市场表现，这也是此次业绩的核⼼看
点。该设备上⽉正式开售，苹果⽅⾯表⽰需求 " ⾮⽐寻常 " ，交货时间已被推迟⾄明年。
Duo 对苹果⽽⾔意义重⼤。它不仅代表着苹果旗舰产品多年来最⼤幅度的设计变⾰，起售价更⾼
达 1999 美元。投资者寄望于这⼀⾼价位能够提振增⻓。据彭博⾏业研究预测， iPhone Duo 在
2027 财年或能贡献 280 亿⾄ 320 亿美元的销售额，并随着消费者从 Pro 及 Pro Max 等低价机型迁
移，推动平均售价进⼀步上扬。
不过，分析师也指出隐忧：供货量有限，加之已有报告显⽰ Duo 屏幕在少量使⽤后出现开裂，可
能会削弱其在早期⽤⼾群体之外的市场吸引⼒。

⾃由现⾦流撑起 " 避⻛港 " 逻辑
与⼤多数⼤型科技同⾏相⽐，苹果的营收增速相对落后，但其⾃由现⾦流表现尤为突出，⽽这
恰恰是当下市场最为看重的指标。在投资者对 AI ⽀出热潮⽇益存疑之际，苹果的现⾦回报能⼒构
成了其核⼼吸引⼒。
据预期，苹果 2026 财年⾃由现⾦流将增⻓逾 40% ⾄ 1415 亿美元，此后三个财年亦预计持续增
⻓。相⽐之下，⾕歌、 Meta 和亚⻢逊均⾯临⾃由现⾦流缺⼝，且预计这⼀状况将持续。
Neuberger Berman ⾼级研究分析师 Daniel Flax 表⽰： " 市场认可苹果在⾃由现⾦流⽅⾯的优
势，如果这⼀势头能够延续，我认为股价从当前⽔平仍有进⼀步创造价值的空间。产品周期的
执⾏能⼒，以及将其转化为强劲⾃由现⾦流增⻓的能⼒，将是决定股价⾛势的最关键因素。⾄
少⽬前来看，初步迹象显⽰ Duo 受到了相当⼤的关注。 "
分析师热情降温，股价已超⽬标价
尽管如此，⾼估值与低增速的组合已令华尔街的热情有所消退。⾃苹果 7 ⽉末上次财报发布以
来，⾄少有六名分析师下调了评级。 Loop Capital 于 10 ⽉将苹果从 " 买⼊ " 下调⾄ " 持有 " ，理由是

iPhone 出货量趋势偏弱；美国银⾏则于上周五将苹果从其 " 美国精选⼀号 " 最佳投资组合中移除，
并表⽰ " 对近期布局持谨慎态度，因其相对于市场⼀致预期存在潜在逆⻛ " 。
在彭博追踪的分析师中，推荐买⼊苹果的⽐例不⾜ 60% ，⽽微软、亚⻢逊和 Meta 的买⼊推荐率
均超过 90%。更值得关注的是，苹果股价已⾼于分析师平均⽬标价，成为 " 七巨头 " 中唯⼀⼀只未
来 12 个⽉隐含回报为负的成员。
Tandem 的 Ben Carew 总结道： " 估值是苹果⾯临的核⼼⻛险，但鉴于其商业模式的确定性相对更
⾼，⼀定程度的估值溢价是合理的。科技板块其他地⽅的波动，恰恰反映了市场对它们前景的
不确定性。除⾮这次财报真正动摇了市场对苹果的基本判断 —— 我认为这种情况不太可能发⽣
—— 否则苹果⾄少应该能对部分波动保持免疫。 "

### Page 3

Document 505: 【趨勢主題】２０２７年美元展望

Source PDF: all report pdf/【趨勢主題】２０２７年美元展望.pdf

### Page 1

2026/9/22
主題報告概述 : 本篇報告將分析 2026年美元現況 ，最終形成
2027年美元看法。以下為本篇報告內容概要:
(1) 匯市現況：美國伊朗戰爭導致通膨疑慮再起，美元走強。
(2) 政治議題：2026年共和黨失去絕對執政對美元的影響。
(3) 期中選後風險因子：財政、貿易戰、美伊戰爭。
(4) 美元基本面：景氣與通膨循環將維持在金髮女孩經濟環境。
(5) 美元技術面：技術面跌破年線，籌碼面偏多但8月轉弱。
(6) 非美匯市: 歐洲政治面利空及日本阻貶為美元提供底部支撐
匯市結論: 美元已從2026年初的降息交易轉向受到美伊戰爭 、

油價上漲及通膨預期升溫支撐 ，但此波強勢更多來自地緣政治
與高利率環境 ，而非美國基本面出現結構性轉強 。期中選舉後 ，
共和黨失去完全執政優勢 ，國會分裂將限制大規模財政擴張 ，
有助抑制通膨與赤字惡化 ，但也降低進一步刺激經濟成長的動
能。展望2027年，美國仍面臨債務上限 、財政協商、貿易政策、
美伊衝突及烏俄戰爭等風險 ，其中地緣政治與油價走勢仍是左
右美元的重要變數 。2027年美元並非進入新一輪強勢多頭週期 ，
而是在高利率支撐下維持區間整理 。2027年美國經濟基準情境
穩健，景氣溫和成長 ，通膨從高檔放緩 。若無重大地緣政治升
級事件，美元指數預期維持97–105區間震盪，並呈現「上有壓
力、下有支撐 」的盤整格局 。當地緣政治升溫時美元偏強 ，當
通膨回落與利率下降時美元偏弱。
資料來源： 中國信託商業銀行整理。2026/9

(1)匯市現況 : 美伊戰爭導致通膨疑慮再起，
美元走強
匯市現況: 2026年美元走勢主要受到美國經濟基本面、Fed貨幣
政策預期、川普政策以及地緣政治風險交互影響。
2026年初，川普重返貿易壁壘政策 、美國就業動能放緩且通膨
趨穩，市場普遍預期降息循環延續 ，使美元維持偏弱整理格局 ，
美元指數於1月27日一度跌至全年低點95.55。期間宣布華許出
任Fed主席的消息曾短暫帶動美元反彈 ，但整體仍未擺脫區間
震盪格局 。然而，2月28日伊朗事件爆發 ，3月9日西德州原油
一度接近每桶 120美元，能源價格上升帶動全球通膨預期升溫 ，
美國降息預期下降。進入3-4月，美國經濟數據進一步展現韌性，
製造業 、消費及投資表現改善 ，Fed季度預測亦同步上修 GDP

成長與通膨展望 。受此影響 ，利率期貨隱含年底前降息預期由
原先降息2碼逐步修正至不降息 ，推動美元於2Q26展開反彈。
同時 ，受油價飆升影響 ，9月Fed季度預測將今年 PCE上修至
3.7%，官員點陣圖亦上修利率路徑 ，市場開始討論通膨壓力可
能迫使Fed重新升息 。匯市焦點亦逐漸轉向新任聯準會主席華
許提出的新貨幣政策框架 。在Fed政策預期持續調整下 ，美元
指數於6月24日升至全年高點101.8，隨後進入高檔盤整格局。

(2)政治議題：2026年共和黨失去絕對執政對
美元的影響(2/3)
數席次機率下行趨勢可見 ，當川普政策面無法政治上快速解決
問題並且影響民生時，取得參眾議院的機率加速下行。
期中選舉結果對美元影響 ：觀察1970年以來美國期中選舉結束
後一年美元之平均表現。
民主黨主導兩院 ：當民主黨期中選舉後在參眾議院均為多數 ，
美元指數平均下跌 -4.6%，6次有4次為下跌 ，且前一年美元均
下跌。1971、1987及2007年為下跌幅度最大的3年。
1971年尼克森(共和黨)總統宣布停止黃金兌換 ，10%進口附加
關稅，迫使盟友重啟貿易與貨幣談判(最終達成史密松寧協定)，
美元下跌-7.8%。

1987年雷根(共和黨)延續1985年廣場協議後美元貶值趨勢延續 。
雙赤字壓力 、通膨升溫 、利率上升以及外資拋售壓力導致發生
1987年10月19日黑色星期一股市崩盤 ，美元指數當年下跌 -
17.6%(1986年下跌-16.1%)。
2007年布希(共和黨)總統時期面臨雙赤字 、非美利差縮小 ，以
及次貸風暴風險升溫。美元下跌-8.3%(2006年下跌-8.2%)。

(2)政治議題：2026年共和黨失去絕對執政對
美元的影響(3/3)
共和黨、民主黨各主導一議院 ：當美國國會進入兩黨各主導一
議院時期，美元指數期中選舉過後一年平均上漲 3.6%，4次有3
次為上漲，且前一年4次美元均為上漲。美國期中選舉若造成國
會由不同政黨掌控 ，通常會限制總統隨意推動大規模擴張性財
政政策與赤字支出 。這類財政節制有助於降低通膨失控風險 ，
進而支撐美元走強。4次國會分裂時段其中較大美元升值趨勢為
1983年雷根(共和黨)時期。 1980年以來聯準會主席保羅伏克爾
採取強力升息手段打擊通膨 ，美國利率保持在相對高檔一段時
間且通膨已逐步受到控制 (從1980年15%降至4%以下)。美元

1983年升值14.9%(1982年升值12.1%)。1983年後，美國經濟
順利擺脫先前的嚴重衰退 ，展開強勁增長 ，市場對美國資產信
心大增，形成所謂強勢美元格局 。美元指數在80年代高點轉折
時期美國實質利率(基準利率-消費者物價指數)已從高檔下降。
小結：2026年期中選舉結果可能形成國會分裂 ，國會分裂長期
有助抑制赤字與通膨 ，因此長期對美元有利 ，但短期可能因預
算協商及債務上限僵局增加政治風險 ，對美元造成波動。2027
年將再度面臨美國債務上限 ，以及美伊地緣政治 、貿易戰的變
數均將牽動實質利率的變化 ，因此接下來將進一步分析選後影
響美元的關鍵因子。
✓

對美元有利
✓

隨美元趨勢走弱

Thematic Report
重點提示
(3)期中選後風險因子：財政、貿易戰、美伊戰
爭(1/3)
期中選舉過後影響美元的關鍵因子聚焦 財政面 (政府關門事件)、
貿易戰(法院裁定關稅無效後宣布以其他方式課徵關稅)、地緣政
治面(伊朗戰爭觸發油價上揚)。
財政面：8月國會通過持續撥款決議至 12月11日，代表政府關
門的風險將落在11月3日後，但新國會於2027年1月3日任職，
因此很可能選後再延後撥款決議 。除此之外 ，根據兩黨政策中
心預測，2027年9月後可能再度面臨美國債務上限議題 ，屆時
面臨2028年預算審核，參眾議院的分治恐延長達成共識的時間
以及提升美國主權債信評或展望調降風險 。從全國納稅人聯盟

(NTU)情境分析預測，美國聯邦政府收支很可能在2027年2月至
7月觸及債務上限。一旦債務達到法定限額，財政部長將宣布暫
停發行債務期 。在暫停發行債務期間內 ，財政部不得增加受法
定限額約束的債務 ，但到期債務仍可展期 。計算美國財政部透
過動用收入 、現有現金儲備和採取非常規措施 ，真正無法履行
聯邦義務約在2028年2月。然而債務上限和債務違約日(X-Date)
何時達到存在很大的不確定性 。受債務上限約束的債務將於
2027年3月接近法定上限 500億美元以內，之後隨著4月稅收的
到來而暫時下降 ，但若政府財政赤字增加超過原先預測 ，X-
Date將會提前到來。

Thematic Report
重點提示
(3)期中選後風險因子：財政、貿易戰、美伊戰
爭(2/3)
美國信評方面 ，惠譽8月將美國信評維持在 AA+評級且展望穩
定，但警告信評下調風險因子如下 ：政府債務佔GDP水準或償
債能力惡化 ，例如未能解決公共支出和收入的平衡 ，或遭受宏
觀金融衝擊 。經濟政策制定可信度下降導致削弱美元儲備貨幣
地位，從而降低政府融資彈性 。惠譽預期 ，美國債務占GDP比
重到2028年可能升至123%，較AA評級國家46.3%的債務比率
中值高出一倍以上 ，因此，美國信評或展望下修的風險有可能
同時伴隨財政惡化或經濟成長放緩 /衰退造成償債能力惡化 。
2027年美國財政議題將繼續面臨逆風 ，共和黨失去完全執政的

環境對財政有利但政治僵局則凸顯債務上限及違約的風險 ，因
此增加美元短期波動風險。
貿易戰：2026年川普貿易戰從對等關稅全面性課徵轉向法律攻
防，預期2027年貿易政策以各國與美國雙邊貿易協定談判為主
軸，以及針對進口品項實施不同的關稅 (川普8月宣布2027年起
對一系列加拿大汽車產品加徵最高達 50%的關稅)。川普貿易政
策不確定性因素延續 ，但本行認為時序進入 2027年貿易戰全面
性惡化的可能性已降低 ，且企業及投資人已對貿易政策不確定
性有一定程度的認知 ，因此傳導至資產價格的影響性亦將有所
降低。
地緣政治 ：地緣政治風險的擴散效應高於上述財政及關稅 。主
因荷姆茲海峽能源輸出量佔全球 20%，油價上漲對通膨的影響
為2027年美元走勢的主要變數 。2月底事件爆發以來原油遠期
曲線現貨溢價 ，代表短期內市場需求強烈或發生供給中斷 。目
前美伊戰爭從熱戰轉向封鎖荷姆茲海峽 。UAE及部分中東國家
已開始運用地面輸油管線避開該管線 ，但伊朗無人機打擊範圍
遠至含蓋紅海 ，倘若事件面惡化 ，紅海運輸除了葉門胡塞武裝

組織干擾，亦有伊朗無人機攻擊風險。目前美伊談判仍未達成
✓
政治僵局凸顯債務
上限及違約風險
，

風險

(3)期中選後風險因子：財政、貿易戰、美伊戰
爭(3/3)
協議，伊朗拒絕交出高濃縮鈾 ，因此仍無法排除美國運用地面
部隊進行搜索 ，也無法排除伊朗透過遠端無人機攻擊美國在其
他中東國家的軍事或煉油設施 ，美國運用地面部隊恐將地緣政
治風險升級，屆時油價、利率以及通膨預期可能同步向上。
風險變數對2027年美元的影響 ：美國政府債務問題並非全新風
險因子。自1960年以來，美國國會已經累計調整法定債務上限
超過78次。然而，2027年若期中選後國會分裂 ，疊加年底預算
勢必面臨再延後撥款決議 ，屆時政府關門或違約的不確定性因
素將影響經濟成長並且降低聯準會緊縮的可能 。而美國債務上

限再度上調則可能引起信評展望調降的風險 。以美元的角度而
言，有紀律的財政計畫的確可將加強投資人對美元資產的信心 ，
對美元有利 。但短線若因此造成政府關門以及無法在預算達成
協議則對美元短線不利 ，因此定調2027年財政風險因子在分裂
國會環境，對美元而言屬於短空長多。
除了債務風險外 ，貿易戰及美伊戰爭事件的發展最終將反應在
油價及通膨的走勢。從今年油價一度飆升至每桶120美元可見，
短期油價的上漲並未造成短線通膨危機。但不可否認，高油價

擴張區間 。伴隨調查及價格指標持續向上 ，整體景氣動能趨勢

正向。 展望2027年，溫和成長伴隨通膨放緩美元處於盤整偏弱
格局，基本情境內貨幣政策則傾向維持基準利率穩定。

義大利民粹政黨執政時期10
年公債殖利率與歐元區利差
30%，歐洲政治生態將進入高度不穩定與兩極化的狀態 。在歐
洲多黨內閣制或雙首長制國家中 ，30%是傳統主流政黨能否順
利執政的關鍵臨界點 。進入2027年，歐盟將有 7國舉行國會選

舉，1月30日德國總統選舉以及4月18日法國總統大選。民粹主
義右翼政黨正在崛起 ，並可能在多個國家大幅增加席次 。每次
選舉結果都將影響各國政治局勢 ，進而反映整個歐洲的政治環
境。然而，右翼民粹主義的崛起對金融市場也並非絕對利空 ，
義大利右翼政黨取得政權後堅持財政紀律 ，向金融市場展現負
責任的治理 ，同時關注移民和文化衝突等社會議題 ，以維繫基
層支持者 。義大利和歐元區 10年期公債利差從義大利右翼政黨
勝選時2.45%至今已收斂至0.83%。此外，許多右翼民粹主義政
黨也緩和了其疑歐立場 ，不再主張退出歐元區或歐盟 。2027年
法國總統大選為首要政治議題。法國極右翼領袖勒龐(Le Pen)與
她所屬的政黨國民聯盟 (RN)近年來調整了政綱 ，已放棄脫歐主
張，並且在財政上淡化激進政策 ，強調財政紀律將遵守歐盟規
範，將財政赤字控制在GDP 3%以下。整體而言，歐洲政治光譜
進入兩極化 ，法國選後議會仍需籌組多數聯盟 ，財政惡化風險
可控，但若極左派梅蘭雄意外勝選，大幅增加政府支出、對富

重點提示
(6)非美匯市 : 歐洲政治不確定性及日本阻貶為
美元提供底部支撐(5/5)
✓
日圓長期轉為多頭
關鍵因子則將落在
日本政府是否開始
運用政策引導海外
投資資金回流
可視為第一波技術面日圓多方關卡。就基本面而言，政府干預匯
市無法改變貨幣政策與財政政策的路徑。若日本通膨無法帶動日
本央行加速升息，日圓利率水準則無法形成資金流入的誘因。以
日圓長期轉為多頭關鍵因子則將落在日本政府是否開始運用政策
引導海外投資資金回流(日本財務省統計到2025年底，日本在海
外的淨資產達近562兆日圓)，將是長期影響日圓走勢的最有效政
策。
匯市結論: 美元已從2026年初的降息交易轉向受到美伊戰爭、油

價上漲及通膨預期升溫支撐 ，但此波強勢更多來自地緣政治與
高利率環境 ，而非美國基本面出現結構性轉強 。期中選舉後 ，
共和黨失去完全執政優勢 ，國會分裂將限制大規模財政擴張 ，
有助抑制通膨與赤字惡化 ，但也降低進一步刺激經濟成長的動
能。展望2027年，美國仍面臨債務上限 、財政協商、貿易政策
及美伊衝突等風險 ，其中地緣政治與油價走勢仍是左右美元的
重要變數 。外部變數則關注烏俄戰爭是否停戰 、美日聯和干預
日圓與日本2028財年赤字規模 ，以及法國總統大選對歐洲政治
光譜的影響。
整體而言，2027年美元並非進入新一輪強勢多頭週期 ，而是在
高利率支撐下維持區間整理 。2027年美國經濟基準情境穩健 ，
景氣溫和成長 ，通膨從高檔放緩 。若無重大地緣政治升級事件 ，
美元指數預期維持 97-105區間震盪，並呈現「上有壓力、下有
支撐」的盤整格局 。當地緣政治升溫時美元偏強 ，當通膨回落
與利率下降時美元偏弱。

景気は弱い動き
中国景気は弱い動きが続いている。9月の製造業PMIは50.1と前月から上昇し、好不況の境目である50を3カ月ぶりに上
回った。構成指数をみると、生産は大きく上昇した一方、就業人員の低下が全体の改善ペースを抑制した。非製造業PMI
は50.2と3カ月ぶりに50を上回ったが、過去と比べると低水準で推移している。なお、2026年4～6月期の実質GDP成長
率は前年比＋4.3%と伸びが鈍化し、季節調整済み前期比（年率換算）も＋3.6%と政府目標の「4.5～5%」を下回った。

9月24日、米国・ワシントンで米中首脳会談が実施された。5月の会談で打ち出された「建設的戦略安定関係」の構築を再

具体的には、米中双方が300億ドル相当の品目で関税引き下げに合意したほか、AI分野で対話枠組みを構築する方針で
合意するなど、個別分野では一定の進展が得られた。もっとも、総じてみると内容は概ね想定内であり、米中対立の全面
的な解消には至っていない。貿易戦争の休戦も今年11月までとされた期限が2カ月延長されたにとどまっており、今後も
両者間で対話を継続することを確認したことが最大の成果だったといえよう。

輸出価格は上昇も、デフレ輸出への懸念はなお強い
中国の輸出価格が足元で上昇している（左図）。2023年以降、EVや太陽光パネル、鉄鋼を中心に輸出価格は下落傾向
にあり、廉価品の輸出が海外市場を席巻する「デフレ輸出」が国際問題となったが、2026年4月以降、状況が一変した。品
目別でみると、世界的に需給が逼迫している集積回路やPC、PC部品の価格上昇が顕著である。

格が下落している（右図）。このため、デフレ輸出への懸念は依然として根強い。

Document 603: 凱基投顧_3711 日月光投控_潘俊宏_20261029

### Page 1

十月 29, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新 IC封裝/測試 ‧ 台灣

日月光投控 
(3711.TW /3711 TT)

過去的上限成為新的起點  
重要訊息 
日月光投控公布強勁的 3Q26 財報，並再度上修 2026 年資本支出計畫。  
評論及分析  
3Q26 財報優於預期， 4Q26 展望樂觀。日月光投控 3Q26 營收季增 28%，
優於公司財測，並優於凱基 預估 4%，係因 LEAP 產能爬升速度快於預期。
毛利率及營益率分別提升至 22.1%及 13.6%，均優於公司 財測、市場共識
及凱基預估，係受惠 LEAP 營收占比提升，以及我們低估公司費用的 控管能
力與營運槓桿效益。稅後淨 利達 270 億元 (稀釋 EPS 為 NT$5.84)，優於凱基
預估 21%。展望 4Q26，公司預期營收將 季增 6-8%，其中，ATM 營收季增
11-13%，EMS 營收季持平，毛利率及營益率則分別提升至 23-24%及 14.5-
15.5%。 
2027 年 LEAP 成長展望樂觀。日月光投控將 2026 年 LEAP 營收財測由 37
億美元上修至超過 40 億美元，並預期 2027 年將倍增。為因應強勁需求，
公司再度上修 2026 年資本支出財測 (由 105 億美元提高至 115 億美元)，並
預期 2027 年資本支出將進一步增加。2027 年 LEAP 營收中，預估 CoWoS
全製程營收將超過 10 億美元，CPO 相關營收達 5 億美元，其餘則來自先進
測試及與晶圓代工夥伴合作的載板上封裝業務。 受限於廠房空間，且 封裝產
能的空間使用效率高於測試，加上測試設備交期較長， 公司預期 2027 年先
進封裝營收成長將快於測試， 並優先將測試 擴產資源投入晶圓測試，而非成
品測試。考量全製程營收將在 4Q26 達到 3 億美元，我們認為 公司對 2027
年超過 10 億美元的營收展望仍 偏保守。隨大部分 CPU 訂單流向日月光，我
們認為潛在需求可達 30 億美元，惟實際營收仍取決於設備交期及產能爬升
良率。我們預估 2027 年 LEAP 營收將達 100-110 億美元 (年增 162%)。 
ATM 毛利率結構性提升。LEAP 業務快速成長將改善 ATM 獲利結構，推升
3Q26 毛利率超越公司 過往提出的 24-30%結構性毛利率區間。儘管 公司未
提供新的毛利率指引，但認為過往 ATM 毛利率區間上緣 (30%) 將成為新的
基準，並預期隨 LEAP 營收占比持續提升，2027 年毛利率將逐季改善。  
投資建議  
考量獲利預估上修及估值基準遞延至 2028 年，我們大幅調升目標價至
1,050 元，仍採用景氣循環高峰本益比 23 倍評價。我們 2026-2028 年 EPS
預估是以完全稀釋股數做計算。 
投資風險  
全球經濟進入衰退；科技產業復甦不如預期 。

4Q26展望 
• 營收季增 6-8%，其中 ATM 營收季增 11-13%，EMS 營收季持平。  
• 毛利率：23 –24%；營利率：14.5–15.5%。 
• ATM 毛利率：31.6 –32.6%，季增 1-2 個百分點。  
• EMS 營利率：2 % 
2026-27年展望 
• 2026 年 LEAP 營收財測由 37 億美元上修至超過 40 億美元，管理層
維持 2027 年 LEAP 營收倍增的預期。  
• 2026 年 CoWoS 全製程業務營收目標維持 3 億美元，進度符合預
期，2027 年營收預期超過 10 億美元。  
• 2026 年主流業務營收預期成長超過 20% (前次財測年增 20%)。 
• 2026 年資本支出財測由 105 億美元上修至 115 億美元，新增 10 億
美元中，5 億美元用於廠房改建，另 5 億美元用於設備，其中 60%
投入 LEAP 業務，40%投入主流業務。

### Page 7

台灣 日月光投控

十月 29, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R 7

ESG 圖表定義  
項目 定義 說明 
能源使用 
直接與間接能源消耗總量(十億焦耳) 
- 公司運營範圍內消耗之能源總量 
- 能源使用總量 = 直接能源消耗總量 + 間接能源消耗量 
- 總能源使用量包括購買的能源、生產的能源 
- 就公用事業而言，輸電/電網損耗為其業務活動之一部分，被視為總能源消耗，數據不包括
為滿足能源使用而生產的電力（公用事業為出售而生產） 
- 就公用事業而言，用於能源生產的煤、天然氣或核能等原料不屬於「總能源使用」項下

購買之再生能源 
Primary再生能源購買總量(十億焦耳) 
- 公司各種來源之能源消耗量與購買的能源中屬於自然界可再生者（太陽能、風能、水能、
生物質能、地熱能）之量  
- 如無證據顯示再生能源由公司生產，則所報告的能源數據視為購買的再生能源

再生能源使用率 再生能源占總能源消耗量之比例  
CO2 約當排放量 
直接 CO2 與 CO2 約當排放量(公噸) 
- 公司擁有或控制的來源的直接排放量（範圍 1 排放量） 
- 相關氣體：二氧化碳 (CO2)、甲烷 (CH4)、一氧化二氮 (N2O)、氫氟碳化物 (HFCS)、全氟化
合物 (PFCS)、六氟化硫 (SF6)、三氟化氮 (NF3)

CO2 約當排放量營收比 
直接 CO2 與 CO2 約當排放量（公噸）銷售（百萬元）比 
- 公司擁有或控制的來源的直接排放量（範圍 1 排放量） 
- 相關氣體：二氧化碳 (CO2)、甲烷 (CH4)、一氧化二氮 (N2O)、氫氟碳化物 (HFCS)、全氟化
合物 (PFCS)、六氟化硫 (SF6)、三氟化氮 (NF3)

廢棄物總量 
廢棄物總量(公噸) 
- 總廢棄物 = 無害廢棄物 + 有害廢棄物 
- 僅考慮固體廢棄物，但如液體廢棄物以公噸為單位呈報，則會將其加入求得包含液體廢棄
物之總量 
- 對於採礦、石油與天然氣等行業，tailings、廢石、煤、飛灰等廢棄物亦考慮在內

廢棄物回收率 
公司呈報的廢棄物回收率 
- 廢棄物回收率 = 廢棄物回收量/總廢棄物*100 
- 廢棄物轉化為能源或經由廢棄物焚燒產生能源視為廢棄物回收 
- 經由堆肥回收的廢棄物視為回收之廢棄物

總取水量 
總取水量(立方公尺) 
- 由呈報組織直接或經水公司等中介機構從任何水源抽取的總水量 
- 水井、城鎮/公用事業/市政用水、河水、地表水等不同之水源均予以考慮

環保支出 
環保支出總金額 
- 所有用於環境保護，或防止、減少、控制環境因素、影響、危害的投資與支出，亦包括處
置、處理、衛生、清理支出

員工流動率 
員工流動率 
- 包括任何原因（自願或非自願）離開公司的員工，如辭職、退休、自然離職/死亡、醫療失
能、冗員、裁員、重組、解僱、裁減或定期合約到期 
- 員工流動率 =（離開之員工/平均員工人數）*100 
- 平均員工人數 =（本年末員工人數 + 去年末員工人數）/2 
- 本財年末員工數 =上個財年末員工數 + 新員工數 - 離開之員工數

女性經理 
女性經理百分比 
- 女性經理占公司經理的百分比 
- 如有不同階層之百分比，如最高階、高階、中階、初階，則會考慮中階女性經理之百分比 
- 女性經理百分比 = 女性經理人數/經理總數*100

女性員工 
女性員工百分比 
- 女性員工占公司員工總數的百分比 
- 女性員工百分比 = 女性人數/員工總數*100

教育訓練總時數 
所有員工教育訓練總時數 
- 僅考慮員工教育訓練時數 
- 包括一般員工所有類型的教育訓練（如健康與安全、環境、急難事件救援、技能與職業發
展） 
- 如果數據以天為單位，則乘以 8，係假設 1 天 =  8 小時工作

每位員工教育訓練時數 平均每年每位員工總教育訓練時數  
資料來源：Refinitiv，凱基

Document 646: 地产专题分析报告：地产成交价格初步止跌

Source PDF: all report pdf/地产专题分析报告：地产成交价格初步止跌.pdf
Report source: 国金证券
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

敬请参阅最后一页特别声明 1

本周新房景气度边际改善，47 城新房成交面积同比回落26.6%，降幅较上周收窄6.1 个百分点。二手房景气度保持平
稳，22 城二手房成交量同比增长10.9%，涨幅好于贴息政策落地前9 月整体水平。10 月末挂牌量较9 月末基本持平，
供给压力整体可控。从更加领先的实时成交数据看，10 月26 个重点城市二手房实时成交套数同比+19%，维持较高热
度。统计局数据显示9 月全国 70 大中城市中，二手住宅销售价格环比上涨城市有 9 个，比上月增加6 个。从更加高
频的周度成交价格指数看，10 月初成交价格较前一周基本持平，初步显现止跌迹象。 
风险提示 
房价下行幅度和速度超预期，宏观经济超预期下行。

### Page 2

宏观经济点评

敬请参阅最后一页特别声明 2

扫码获取更多服务 
新房方面，本周（10.24-10.30）景气度边际改善，47 城新房成交面积同比回落 26.6%，降幅较上周收窄 6.1 个百分
点。其中，一线城市同比增长7.7%，二线、三四线城市同比分别下降33.2%、34.0%。 
二手房方面， 本周 （10.24-10.30） 景气度保持平稳，22 城二手房成交量同比增长10.9%， 涨幅好于贴息政策落地前9
月整体水平。从代表供给的挂牌量看，10 月末26 个重点城市挂牌量较9 月末基本持平，供给压力整体可控。从更加
领先的实时成交数据看，10 月26 个重点城市二手房实时成交套数同比+19%，维持较高热度，预计成交热度有望延续
至11 月。

来源：ifind，国金证券研究所 
从价格层面看， 根据国家统计局数据，9 月一线城市二手住宅销售价格环比上涨0.2%， 涨幅比上月扩大0.1 个百分点。
二线城市二手住宅销售价格环比下降0.2%， 降幅比上月收窄0.1 个百分点。 三线城市二手住宅销售价格环比下降0.3%，
降幅与上月相同。70 个大中城市中， 二手住宅销售价格环比上涨城市有9 个， 比上月增加6 个。从更加高频的周度成
交价格指数看，10 月初全国成交价较前一周基本持平，初步显现止跌迹象。

宏 观 ： 国 内 P M I 回 落 ， 海 外 紧 缩 升 温宏 观 ： 国 内 P M I 回 落 ， 海 外 紧 缩 升 温
期货研究报告
发布时间：2026.10.31
适用周期：2026.10.31~2026.11.7
浙商期货有限公司期货交易咨询业务资格：证监许可[2011]1287号
【撰写人】浙商期货有限公司 郑弘（Z0015227）
【咨询电话】0571-87215357
一、观点综述
核心结论：美联储、欧洲央行与日本央行10月议息会议集体按兵不动但整体偏鹰，12月再度加息预期升温；10月30日美股科技巨头财报不及预期引发
大跌，拖累全球风险资产，国际金价跌破4000美元/盎司创近一年新低，国际油价因霍尔木兹海峡油轮遇袭大涨；国内10月制造业PMI回落至49.3%重
回收缩区间，经济景气阶段性走弱，A股震荡下行、上证指数失守3600点。
逻辑链条：
① 海外央行偏鹰：美联储10比2维持利率不变，欧央行维持2.50%，日本央行8比1维持1.25%，12月加息概率约七成。

② 海外市场：科技财报暴雷引发纳指单日-2.86%，金价跌破4000美元，WTI涨至104.12美元。
③ 国内数据：10月制造业PMI 49.3%、较上月-0.8个百分点，非制造业49.7%同样回落，上证指数-2.48%。
风险提示：12月海外央行集体加息预期与全球利率高位；科技股调整与AI资本开支预期回落引发流动性踩踏；中东局势与油价大涨推升输入性通胀；国内经济景气阶段性回落与A股风险偏
好走弱。
上周资产变化
数据说明：最新为10月30日收盘或时点值；A股与商品为10月30日收盘，美股指数为10月30日美股收盘，变化为最新值较窗口首个交易日10月23日；收益率数值上升标红、下降标绿，USDCNY数
值下降代表人民币升值并标绿。

二、国内经济情况
注：9月工业增加值、社会消费品零售总额、固定资产投资与三季度GDP已于10月19日公布，9月物价与金融数据于10月14日至15日公布，9月进出口于10月13日公布；10月采购经理指数于10月
31日公布，为窗口内唯一新公布数据。

总量数据：三季度GDP同比+4.5%、环比+1.2%，前三季度累计+4.6%，落在全年目标区间内；GDP平减指数连续两个季度为正。
2. 国内社消零售
9月社零当月同比+1.1%、较8月回升0.7个百分点，主要受中秋假期前置于9月的节日消费带动，粮油食品类+6.7%、烟酒类与餐饮收入同步抬升；通讯器材类+31.1%
受益于数码新品集中上市，汽车类-21.2%降幅继续扩大。1-9月服务零售额同比+5.0%，继续强于商品消费。

### Page 6

3. 固定资产投资
1-9月固投累计同比-7.2%、降幅与前值持平，9月环比转正增长0.07%；制造业投资累计-2.1%、降幅收窄0.2个百分点，为4月以来首次收窄，基建-4.2%小幅走弱，房
地产-20.6%降幅扩大0.7个百分点。地产端新开工-25.0%、竣工-25.1%，投资端压力仍在累积。

### Page 9

4. 进出口情况
9月以美元计价出口+36.3%、进口+31.0%，前三季度进出口总额同比增长18.8%；出口分项同步更新至9月，自动数据处理设备+93.3%、高新技术产品+63.9%、汽
车+61.9%，AI相关电子链与外需韧性共同构成主要拉动。

### Page 12

5. 工业增加值
9月规模以上工业增加值当月同比+5.5%、较8月加快0.3个百分点，增速连续两个月抬升；制造业增长6.0%基本持平，采矿业由负转正至1.3%，公用事业加快至5.6%。
新动能产品产量维持较快增长，工业机器人+18.9%、新能源汽车+16.9%、集成电路+18.4%，均为当月产量同比口径。

### Page 15

6. 国内价格指标
9月CPI当月同比+0.9%、与8月持平，核心CPI+1.0%；PPI当月同比+5.0%、较8月回升1.2个百分点并创年内新高，环比上涨1.2%，前三季度累计同比+2.3%。价格修
复延续，通胀环境较上半年明显好转。

### Page 18

7. 金融数据
9月社融存量同比+7.1%、较8月回落0.1个百分点，M2同比+7.2%、回落0.3个百分点，M1同比+3.2%、回落0.9个百分点，M2与M1剪刀差走阔至4.0个百分点。10月
贷款市场报价利率维持不变，1年期3.0%、5年期以上3.5%，为连续第17个月持平；央行10月MLF净回笼2000亿元。

### Page 21

8. 景气指数
10月官方制造业PMI 49.3%、较9月回落0.8个百分点并重回收缩区间，受十一假期前部分需求提前释放以及国际环境复杂多变等因素影响，供需两端均有放缓，生产指
数与新订单指数分别为49.8%与49.4%；高技术制造业与装备制造业PMI分别为51.3%与50.6%，继续高于临界点，新动能仍保持较快增长。非制造业商务活动指数
49.7%、回落0.5个百分点，服务业商务活动指数50.0%持平荣枯线。财新制造业PMI仍为9月的52.1%。

### Page 22

三、海外经济情况

三季度GDP初值于10月29日公布。
